ParlaMint-DK_20160329130011	The meeting is open.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329130020	This is the first meeting since former Prime Minister Anker Jørgensen died on 20 March 2016. Anker Jørgensen was born in 1922 in Sundby on Amager. Anker Jørgensen became an orphan at a very early age. Hans father died when he was 2 years old and his mother when he was 5 years old. He then grew up with his aunt at Christianshavn. Anker Jørgensen left school at the age of 14 and worked many years as an unskilled worker. But even though Anker Jørgensen had only a short schooling, he spent his whole life studying and further education. Among other things: He studied 3 months at Harvard University in the United States. Anker Jørgensen became early politically active, and his political career seriously took hold of a 1950 when he was elected Vice-President of the Warehouse and Pakhus Workers' Federation, for which he became President in 1956. Later, the Chairmanship of the Transport Group followed in 1962 and the Chairmanship of the Special Workers' Union in 1968. Anker Jørgensen was elected to the Danish Parliament in 1964 and he was a member for 30 years. He was chairman of the Social Democratic Party from 1972 to 1987. In 1972 he replaced Jens Otto Krag as Prime Minister. As Prime Minister from 1975 to 1982, Anker Jørgensen was able to form four governments. On 3 September 1982 he resigned without holding elections and was replaced on 10 September by the Conservative Poul Schlüter in the post. Anker Jørgensen, with his popular and open-minded appearance, had great credibility in the people. He was faithful throughout his life to his upbringing and the ideals of his youth. As Prime Minister, he preferred to stay in his three-bedroom apartment at Borgbjergsvej in South Harbour and was rarely at Marienborg. Anker Jensen was an honorable, sympathetic and above all soilful person, with whom the whole of Denmark was first-named. Honor Anker Jørgensen's memory!

ParlaMint-DK_20160329133402	The background to the call for this question debate is, of course, to clarify whether the government is willing to put an end to the illegal practices that, in our opinion, are obviously taking place with Uber's introduction to the taxi industry. We believe that an effort is needed now and here, and it has nothing to do with a future taxi legislation, which has, as it were, been used as an attempt to apologise for dragging things out and that nothing really happens. We must of course be of the opinion that the legislation in force at all times, old and new, must be respected, and so right now we have the legislation that we have, which places very great demands on the taxi industry and on those who want to establish themselves as road hauliers or drive as drivers. It's a requirement for permission to drive at all. It is a demand for economics for the road hauliers. It is more stringent requirements for cars and their equipment in order to increase safety when transporting people around. It is a requirement for extended insurance. These are requirements for the training of both road hauliers and drivers; it is not enough just to have a driving licence. And there are many other demands for the industry. The Danish Road Traffic Authority had no doubt that with Uber there was an illegal taxi business, where neither the company nor the drivers comply with the many rules in the area, and in any case Uber reported almost the same second, it can be said that they established themselves, and this happened on 19 November 2014 in a notification to the Copenhagen Police, but only on 17 March 2015 was Uber charged with taxi offences, while Uber drivers continued to drive people from A to B with no permission and without complying with the requirements of professional passenger transport. Only towards the end of the year last year, the turn of 2015/2016, the police in Copenhagen began to charge Uber drivers with pirate driving, and the first cases come before the courts here at the end of April. It must be said that it has taken a very long time for the authorities to get into gear, and one cannot be free from the suspicion that it is quite conscious, bearing in mind that Uber has been embraced by liberalist politicians in the government and among the support parties, who, after all, hail deregulation and regard trade unions and agreements as something created by a certain master. Of course, this slowness has caused great frustration among drivers and hauliers in the taxi companies. They have run authorities and politicians on the doors and used all available legal means to shout us o. They would very much like to see action. Their sense of justice is obviously violated when they see their livelihoods taken out of their mouths without any action being taken. It happens every weekend, every day virtually, and that is something that perhaps the Minister of Justice in particular should take quite seriously. Even the European Union has been used as an excuse for doing nothing. This is happening, among other things. in a reply to me from the Minister for Justice, referring to the fact that a Spanish court has requested an assessment by the European Court of Justice of the legality of Uber's activities. But that is not all that the Court of Justice has the right to interfere in, and I would remind the Minister that Danish legislation and the related rules are a purely national matter, and if you run your business in violation of the law, it is illegal. I hope that the ministers will live up to this if the European Court of Justice once again fucks up and interferes with something that it should not interfere with, and that the government then does not use the European Union as a bad excuse for not taking action against the illegal practices that are taking place right under our noses.

ParlaMint-DK_20160329133735	Thank you. I have been asked to tell you what can be said about the taxi service Uber, and I am sure you can say more than I do here, but it is certainly an orientation that comes here and now. Uber was introduced in Copenhagen on 19 November 2014. On the same day, the Danish Transport and Construction Agency asked the Copenhagen Police to investigate whether Uber was violating the taxi law. And this was because, in the opinion of the Danish Transport and Construction Authority, there was so-called commercial passenger transport which requires a permit, in this case a taxi permit. In the case of commercial passenger transport, there is no link between the passenger and the driver. As the Minister of Justice will discuss, on 17 March 2015 the police charged Uber with violation of the taxi law. What has happened in this case since the charges, the Minister of Justice will explain. Copenhagen Airport has also reported Uber to the police to use airport parking spaces to take passengers. In the following I will outline Uber as a company and how they operate. Uber states that they are an IT company which only provides an app free of charge to those who wish to participate in Uber's driving event. Uber informs us that this is a co-driving operation. Uber also reports that they do not own cars or employ drivers. The service operates so that users can download Uber's app on their smartphone free of charge and potential customers can contact people running for Uber in this way. I have also been informed that it is Uber who sets and regulates the price of driving, for example. also comes with various offers that the cars must follow if they drive at certain times or whatever may be involved. The payment is made electronically in such a way that the money is transferred directly from the customer's account to Uber, which then forwards part of the payment to the driver. There are therefore no payment transactions between the user and the driver. Uber's withholding part of the amount. The amount which will then be paid to the driver must be borne by the driver in respect of petrol, maintenance, etc. too. The driver must also arrange for the payment of tax. I have noted that the TV newspaper on 6 December 2015 featured a feature where Uber's director among others. reported that over the last month 1,000 drivers had served approx. 35,000 customers. I and my officials have also been approached on a regular basis by a group of hauliers and drivers in the metropolitan area who have set out to register how many wagons are driving for Uber. They have come with figures of approximately the same size. It was also stated here that the payment transactions are carried out by debiting the payment on users' debit cards and entering into an account with Uber. The official scheme should be for drivers to be paid 80%. of the customer's payment, but there are drivers who drive or have run for Uber, who state that the tariff varies from 60 to 80%. depending on demand. The driver himself must pay the tax on the income. Today Uber operates in about 70 big cities scattered around the world and starts in new cities at a great pace. The company has met with very different reactions in the countries. The authorities of Norway and Sweden believe that driving on the basis of Uber's concept requires authorisation. The authorities have therefore in several cases imposed fines and removed car license plates. Uber has also been met with bans in e.g. Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329134150	Thank you, Minister. Then it's the Minister of Justice for an answer.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329134157	My share in this case, as the Minister for Transport and Building briefly pointed out, is the criminal prosecution of Uber drivers and Uber. As I said earlier, I am aware that the criminal proceedings against Uber have been suspended. However, the prosecution is in full swing and, as you know, the first cases against Uber drivers will be brought to court on 26 April of this year. I will briefly explain the prosecution's handling of the case against the company Uber. As the Minister for Transport and Building has just explained, the Danish Police Department reported on 19 November 2014 Uber for violation of taxi law. On 29 January 2015, the Copenhagen Police Department contacted Uber and reported that a criminal investigation had been initiated against the company. The Copenhagen Police Department carried out investigations into the case, and there were, among other things, investigations into the case. Interrogations were carried out by a number of persons who had signed up as Uber drivers. On 17 March 2015, charges were brought against Uber for complicity in violation of the Taxi Act. The charge is therefore that Uber will help people, i.e. Uber drivers, to carry out commercial passenger transport services through Uber's electronic platform without having the necessary authorisation under taxi law. In June 2015, Uber's lawyer announced that Uber would make himself available for an interrogation in August 2015, and the Copenhagen Police sent a number of questions to Uber to form the basis for the hearing. It was then agreed that Uber should reply to the questions in writing. On 28 September 2015, Uber explained the company's business concept to Copenhagen Police. In this regard, it was stated that Uber alone provides a technology platform and that Uber does not believe that Uber is covered by Danish taxi legislation. In this regard, Uber indicated that a case was pending before the European Court of Justice around Uber. The Copenhagen Police were thus informed during the proceedings that a Spanish judge has requested a preliminary ruling from the European Court of Justice in a Uber case. It should also be noted that a Belgian case concerning Uber is also pending before the European Court of Justice. I informed you in the autumn of 2015 in the reply of 8 October 2015 to Question No. 70 of the Committee on Transport and Building to the Danish Parliament that the prosecution in this respect considered whether an indictment could be made against the company in this country or whether there was a need to await clarification of the EU legal issues raised in relation to Uber's business concept. The question has been discussed in detail at a meeting between the Attorney General and the Ministry of Justice at the end of February. The attorney general is now assessing, among other things. on the basis of the meeting in question, that the above-mentioned EU legal questions do not prevent the prosecution of Uber for complicity in the infringements of taxi law by specific drivers. The Copenhagen Police Department has reported that until 15 March a total of 33 persons have been charged with infringements of taxi legislation in connection with Uberdrift. The Copenhagen Police Department has also stated that the police department continues to raise charges for infringements of the taxi law when it is found that these are Uberdrifts. As I have said, the prosecution has now indicted six of the cases, which are scheduled for trial in court at the end of April. The reason why only six of the cases have been indicted is, as I understand it, that the prosecution has brought these cases to trial. The prosecution will therefore await the outcome of these proceedings before taking a decision on the other charges. Like many others, I look forward to matters being settled. The cases can then be appealed to the High Court, so it is far from certain that we will have a final clarification at the end of April. I understand from the prosecution that it will also await the outcome of the six cases before proceeding with the case against Uber for complicity in the violation of the taxi law by specific drivers. It is the professional judgement of the Public Prosecutor that it is essential that drivers who are now indicted may be required to testify in connection with the case against Uber, and this can only be done once the proceedings against drivers have been completed. As I have said, I am quite clear that clarification of whether or not Uber drivers' passenger services are actually lawful has been withdrawn, but at the same time I also accept that the prosecution must have considered the matter thoroughly and that it has dealt with the issues raised by the case and, as I have said, I am pleased that the first cases against Uber drivers now have to be brought to justice so that we can clarify the legal situation.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329134652	Thank you, Minister. Then it's mr. Henning Hyllested as rapporteur for the proposers. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329134710	Thank you for the statements by the respective ministers. In particular, I found the Minister for Justice's statement interesting. It has to be said that something seems to be happening now, but there is also an admission that the matter has been pulled out smoothly. In our view, Uber is, in fact, the worst disappointment of social dumping. These are the salaries of drivers to well below the level of the collective agreement. There are no health and safety rules in terms of working time requirements. On the contrary, it is a question of people driving in their spare time after a full day's work, or it is people on transfer income who supplement the income with a little black money from pirate driving. Requirements for cars for high safety standards and equipment are not met, including the additional requirements for taxis insurance. The requirement for authorisation to operate professional passenger transport services and the requirement of good economics, unblemished criminal record, training and driver cards has been infringed. Nothing is under control. Consumer safety, which is intended to ensure a professional and safe product and protect the consumer, is not affected. We are convinced that this is a black economy in which neither Uber, as a company nor drivers, pay taxes, because there is no reporting obligation on Uber with regard to the income of drivers, and so a model of companies designed for tax purposes is also used with branches in tax havens around the world. We are quite clear that there are forces in this country, in the government and in some of the support parties who want deregulation in the best superliberal style a la CEPOS: Away with the rules to protect customers from exorbitant prices and provide a certain level of safety; away with health and safety rules and tough requirements to transport other people around; in with free wage formation without restrictive and laborious agreements and with black economy. To expose a whole industry of hard-working drivers and hauliers to unfair and unfair competition in the sacred name of liberalism is quite an achievement. I can hear that ordinary taxi drivers, with whom I sometimes drive, say that they can feel that earnings have fallen between DKr 2 000 and DKr 4 000. on a weekend, and that is especially where there are people to ride and where you have to have the bulk of your earnings, but where the Uberpirates are also on the street. In fact, there are some people who are trying to make a living here. And it has to be worthwhile to work not true but not knowing that you are driving a little extra taxi in your spare time when you have come home from your normal job. Professional passenger transport, which involves a high level of responsibility for the safe arrival of passengers, should not be a leisure job for school teachers or a supplement to cash benefits. Uber has launched itself as part of the growing share economy, a so-called co-driving project. In our opinion, nothing is more wrong. Uber is neither more nor less co-driving than regular taxi services. It is quite clearly defined in the tax order what can be described as "co-driving', namely that no remuneration is received for driving, but that only the costs of direct driving, i.e. petrol and oil, can be taken into account. Conversely, it is also clearly defined what commercial passenger transport is, in other words, what taxi drivers provide. There must be no closer link between the driver and the passenger and a remuneration is received. That is why, of course, the same rules must also apply to those who want to carry people from A to B, and that is just what the established taxi industry demands, namely equal conditions. The taxi industry is a well-regulated industry with very high demands on hauliers and drivers and their cars. It provides a safe and professional product. The Enhedslisten is in favour of this and is very sceptical about liberalisations and deregulations, because it always ends up being a competition for employees' pay and working conditions in this case, but also for drivers' pay and working conditions. Every place where taxi driving has been liberalised has led to more cars on the street, less profits for the individual taxi and also a lack of transparency in prices. I have tried it myself in Sweden since they had just completed the full liberalisation over there, and that was only because heartfelt dockworkers in Gothenburg helped us not to be cheated, so the water drifted, to put it plainly. That is why a great many places, and also in Sweden, have partly rolled back liberalisation. Let us get things done in an industry that has had tough times since the beginning of the crisis in 2008 and which is already struggling with a half-baked image. Let us combat social dumping, working poor, cheating and black economy where it occurs, and here it is in the taxi industry. Don't let Uber ruin a good idea, as part-economics and co-driving is, of course, by adorning himself with borrowed feathers. Therefore, on behalf of the Socialist People's Party, Enhedslisten and Dansk Folkeparti, I have to submit the following: Suggested adoption The Danish Parliament notes that the Danish Traffic Authority (now the Danish Transport and Construction Authority) has already notified Uber on 19 November 2014 of the infringement of taxi driving law to Copenhagen Police. The Danish Parliament also notes that on 17 March 2015 the Copenhagen Police have filed charges against Uber for violation of the taxi driving law, and several accused Uber drivers will be brought to trial in April 2016. Against this background, the Danish Parliament is instructing the government to set up a Task Force Uber with the participation of tax authorities, the Danish Transport and Construction Authority and the police with the aim of carrying out a special effort to ensure that conditions around Uber are brought into line with taxi law. This means, among other things, that Uber drivers are registered as taxi drivers/truckmen with authorisation, vanity and economics requirements, car design and equipment, training and driver cards, etc. as required by the Taxi Driving Act. Part of the special effort is targeted at pirate driving, which is carried out by inter alia: The Uber drivers. • V 41).

ParlaMint-DK_20160329143851	Thank you for that, President. I would like to begin by thanking the Enhedslisten for asking this question. I think it is a good thing that we are debating the situation of passenger transport in Denmark, as this is a matter that we will be working on a great deal in the future. Let me start by making it clear that the Left wants to take care of the labour market. Furthermore, we want fair competition between our private companies. This also applies to commercial passenger transport, also known as the taxi industry. We have looked forward to this debate on Uber, where two things should, however, be distinguished: firstly, the pending case of Uber's legality with regard to taxi law and, secondly, the future legal framework for passenger transport. First of all, I would like to make a few comments on the pending case. Uber was introduced in Copenhagen on 19 November 2014. On the same day, the Danish Transport and Construction Agency asked the Copenhagen Police to clarify whether Uber was violating the taxi law. The reason is that it requires a permit to carry out passenger transport on payment from customers, also known as commercial passenger transport. It is a clear task for the police and courts to ensure the enforcement of applicable law in Denmark. On 17 March 2015, Uber was charged by the police with violation of the Taxi Code. The Minister of Justice has already explained the status and further progress of the case. In the Left, we are very much looking forward to finally having a court settlement within a little month, precisely on 26 April. I am sure that a great many others, other than us, are looking forward to a long-awaited clarification, including the many frustrated taxi drivers and frustrated taxi drivers. As I said, I see it as a standing task for the police and courts to ensure compliance with applicable legislation in close cooperation with all relevant authorities. With regard to the forward-looking legal framework for passenger transport, I would like to make the following point: there is an urgent need for more modern legislation on passenger transport. There is a need to ensure good taxi coverage both in cities and in the countryside. It's about a Denmark in balance. The pervasive taxi death must be stopped. In this context, taxi coverage is needed in the strict context of public service traffic, Flextrafik and local bus transport. These are connected vessels. In addition, there is an urgent need to make room for innovation and innovation in the passenger transport industry. The aim is to ensure space for new development opportunities and efficiency for the benefit of customers and for the industry itself. Reasonable use of new knowledge and technology has historically contributed to more prosperity in Denmark. It is therefore very satisfactory that the government intends to put forward a proposal for modernising the taxi law following the necessary political process, in order to ensure precisely room for innovation and innovation, in other words a controlled deregulation of the taxi market. In this context, it is essential that we ensure a sound legal basis for fair competition, for orderly conditions, for compliance with tax legislation and for passenger safety. A means of this could be the introduction of a new universal licence, in which we politically define clear, basic minimum requirements for those who want to make money from transporting people. There is nothing unpleasant about making money out of it, but then you have to live up to clearly defined minimum requirements. I look forward to a political debate on all this in the coming weeks, and I would like to put forward the following (on behalf of V, LA, RV and KF): Proposals for adoption The Danish Parliament wishes to see the labour market dealt with, including in the taxi industry. The Danish Parliament notes that the first cases against drivers who have run on the basis of communication from Uber are due to be brought before the court on 26 April 2016 and in this context looks forward to a clarification as to whether these journeys comply with taxi law. Against this background, the authorities will consider further efforts to ensure compliance with applicable law. Exploitation of new knowledge and technology has historically resulted in increased prosperity. Therefore, the Danish Parliament welcomes the Government's intention to make proposals for modernising taxi legislation in order to ensure room for innovation and new technologies in the field of commercial passenger transport in a market characterised by fair competition, compliance with tax legislation and safety for passengers. • V 43).

ParlaMint-DK_20160329151743	The question today is very specific about Uber and what the government wants to do to get rid of illegal taxi driving. It is a very specific issue which unfortunately also takes for granted that Uber and the car owners who use Uber are breaking the law. We have not been fully clarified yet, whether or not to what extent and in what way they do so. Unfortunately, this also makes the debate a little cold, because we lack an important, firm piece in what we will get at least part of in a small month's time. However, it may also help to raise our eyes a little and discuss some of the issues which, in my view, cannot be separated from the discussion about Uber. What size is Uber? Is it part economy, or is it a gig economy? Is that taxi driving? And how do we deal with it as a society and parliament, when technology and global digitalisation have a direct impact on Danish citizens, Danish employees and entire industries? I do not think that Uber has anything to do with shared economy, as I see it, and let me be clear straight away: Delekonomi is such a thing as GoMore, if we talk about transport, where a car owner has to go from one place to another and turn up the trip to give others the opportunity to come cheaply from A to B and even save on the trip. At the same time, it reduces potential congestion if only one of the passengers would otherwise have driven himself. Deløkonomi can also be a common minibus in a village or when a road guild is shared about a garden tractor, and I am crazy about deløkonomi that makes up the idea that we should all own it all when it can actually be better to share it. But that's not what Uber is all about. Uber makes it possible to organise passenger transport, particularly in large cities, but in the long term also around the country, in a flexible and fast way. In principle, services such as Uber can make us use resources wiser, but unfortunately, as things stand at present, Uber also offers far too much scope for avoiding tax payments, and there is absolutely no security and security for either driver or passengers in a taxi. There is no doubt that there are some major problems in terms of how we are to deal with those challenges, but they need to be addressed. Uber or, for that matter, other digital communication platforms such as Airbnb or Fiverr are in strong advance all over the world, and this certainly does not mean that we should just lay down flat for them and accept their entry into the Danish market without keeping them accountable for whether they comply with Danish legislation. For of course, players on Danish soil must comply with Danish law, but if Danish law is not geared to or prepared for the new digital platforms, we will also have to look at whether the law is sufficiently secure for the future. If we stand with our arms crossed and want to ban everything we do not already know and come to Denmark from abroad, then we will have a very closed and very little innovative country. Instead, we should look at the challenges facing the taxi industry in this case, while at the same time daring to see the benefits that can be found in the new technologies. The same technologies can also be used to distribute resource consumption sensibly, provide better opportunities to choose sustainable solutions and give people with a more loose connection to a field of work an opportunity to unfold. When the whole way we work is breaking up and more and more people choose to piece together a working life from different activities, we should look more closely at how to ensure working conditions, pensions and safety for employees and those who combine to be employees and self-employed, rather than going hard to shut it down. In my view, this is done in a wise dialogue with the industries and public players, who, as things stand now, account for half the turnover of traditional taxi services, and of course also the new players in the market, who come with experience and skills from other countries. The alternative therefore believes that we as legislators should be proactive and listen to both the taxi industry and Uber when we look at how taxi legislation should be updated. Finally, to return to the question itself, I would like to stress that, in the alternative, we are of course of the opinion that companies operating on Danish soil must comply with Danish legislation, no matter how large, new and innovative they are. We also think that we have included this in the text of the adoption that we have made together with the Socialists.

ParlaMint-DK_20160329152520	In the Radical Left, we want a new, modern taxi law that allows new companies to enter the market for the benefit of innovation, for the benefit of consumers and for the benefit of society. In fact, the taxi law we have forbids the creation of new companies, however strange it may seem. You can't actually drive a taxi in company form at all. It has to be one man, one car, one permit. This is a bit like saying: Congratulations on getting a carpenter's letter, but just don't do a carpenter's business, for those who are already there must be allowed to be the only ones who are there. We think that's weird. It is also the case that a number of different permits are currently being used. For example: you can have a car that gets permission to drive patients to the hospital, but then you can't use that car Saturday night to drive people to and from the city because it's another permit. The SR government proposed, on the basis of a committee work in which everyone in the industry was involved, to create a new taxi law which made it possible to operate a taxi in company form and which also made one permit, a universal permit, which could then be granted to drive a taxi or drive children to school or patients in hospital. At that time, we could not gather 90 seats behind it, and the main reason why we are today helping to put forward a proposal for adoption with the three bourgeois parties is that we have been opened up, unlike two years ago, to create a new taxi law. We think that is needed. In such a new taxi law there must of course be that a new company with some extra cars, or whatever you want to put into the market, must have a permit. And the challenge with Uber, which we are discussing today, is that they do not have any permission to drive, as it is right now, and of course you must have that. The challenge today is that you cannot obtain a permit, because you have no right to do so. I think you should have that. And then, of course, there must be such a permit that there must be insurance, that the person must have a driving licence, that the car is not falling apart, and you can include environmental requirements, health and safety requirements and all kinds of requirements, just as we do in relation to all other industries. But the basic thing about not being allowed to set up as a taxi company is that we think we are wrong and we want to change that. And I think that is the basic challenge in this debate, that is to say that we cannot set up a new taxi company and thus create any competition for any new thinking on the market. After all, this debate is likely to end in a number of proposals for adoption, as it is so technically called, for which there is not really a majority. But we hope that at some point we can agree on a new taxi law whereby Uber and other new companies can be regulated so that they can become part of society rather than the opposite. We hope that this can end at some point in this debate.

Then, when an actor comes who is not subject to these expensive demands, we can understand that it is perceived as competition on unequal terms. The forthcoming revision of taxi legislation must therefore ensure fair and equal competition. We are not going to be in favour of making the same demands on Uber as we are on the taxis today, because then it is no longer Uber. Then their business model can't work and hang together. However, we will be in favour of making equally strict demands on the various players on the market, not on the same requirements, but on equally strict requirements. It goes without saying, of course, that Uber has to pay taxes. Anything else is an unacceptable position. We imagine that some of the demands for taxis can be relaxed, the taxi monopoly abolished and legislation introduced specifically for services such as Uber. We look forward to the forthcoming work on making this legislation and hope that we will finish this year, as we are well aware that there is currently a very great frustration in the taxi industry that we must address.

Just as we must never be blind to the lack of equality that exists in certain ethnic settings, we must not forget the fundamental challenges of equality that are particularly important in the labour market, and that is, in particular, the pay gap between men and women and the great inequality in pension savings and discrimination. Those things are still real in today's Denmark. We need a fight with the gender-based labour market and educational choices, because this means that people are still talking about men's and women's occupations, and in the years to come Denmark will need many more skilled skilled workers. This means that we need more female craftsmen and that we need more male educators and SOSUs. Lack of equal pay is perhaps one of the greatest challenges facing us in terms of equality. A pay gap of 17%. between men and women is not good enough, and that means that we must take responsibility from the political side. So an important tool to get rid of this pay gap is greater transparency about wage statistics in the workplace. Unfortunately, the government's roll-back of gender-based pay statistics for small businesses means that hundreds of thousands of employees in Denmark are no longer covered. This is a major setback in an area where we need progress. Inequality in pay has been constant over the past many years, and instead of simply rolling back sensible legislation, we call on the government to take the problem more seriously and launch new positive initiatives. That there is not full equality in the Danish labour market is seen both on the floor of the labour market and in the boardrooms. The Social Democratic Party would like to welcome the Minister's and the government's support for the Danish model for more women in management. We are pleased that the Left has changed course. But we must also recognise that we are far from achieving this. Although the legislation that has been put into effect benefits businesses, when there is also equality at the top, we must, on the political side, keep a close eye on this area and, of course, we must ensure that positive developments continue. Working life and family life are also closely linked, and a strong welfare society has made Denmark one of the world's best countries to start a family. The possibility of going on maternity leave is absolutely central, but it is also an area where, unfortunately, equality is lagging behind. When men take only a very small part of parental leave or perhaps none at all, it is important for their relationship with their children, just as in the main picture it puts women at a disadvantage in the labour market. That is why the Social Democratic Party shares the Government's ambition that men should take a greater share of parental leave. But one thing is ambition, another is action. This is precisely where the government is acting contrary to its own objectives. This was done, among other things, when it was decided to roll back the maternity allowance for self-employed workers. The roll-back of this legislation means that 200,000 self-employed people no longer have the same maternity opportunities as the rest of the workforce. It is a step in the wrong direction if we want to ensure more equality in the labour market, just as it is the wrong way if we want to see more women entrepreneurs. On the part of the Social Democratic Party, we would like to urge the government to take this challenge more seriously and as soon as possible call the parties of the Danish Parliament for negotiations on how to ensure proper maternity conditions for self-employed people, for which the majority of the Danish Parliament, the opposition and the Danish People's Party, have clearly expressed their desire. Social democracy wants to promote equality between fathers and mothers, and that is why we also want to see an end to the procedures in the public sector where parents are not equal. There are, unfortunately, numerous examples of the fact that only the mother receives public mail concerning the child. Of course, both the father and the mother must have information on equal terms when it comes to their common children. Therefore, we would also like to sign for the government to highlight digital mail to both parents as a good example of how to strengthen equality between parents. That is the path we must continue along. The next step is the payment of the children's check, as today per year. automatic payment to the mother. This testifies to a somewhat outdated and misconception that the mother still disappears like that. automatic is the primary provider and the primary caregiver of the child. We're gonna have to do this automatic. There must be no more "A" and "B" parents in Denmark, and the public must therefore take the lead. That is why we also welcome the fact that the government will now investigate the area and we will be even more pleased if it is followed by action. There is much we can do to increase equality in Denmark, but unfortunately political decisions are also being taken that are moving in the wrong direction. The social democracy was opposed when the government, together with the Danish People's Party, adopted its new cash aid ceiling. We were against it because we saw it as an expression of an unfair and biased policy, because it also had very clear effects on equality. In addition to the fact that it hits vulnerable people hard, it also hits equality. Seven out of ten who want to feel this new cash-aid ceiling, so feel it, so it hurts... are women. The group of single parents... it's especially single mothers who are in this group... will lose an average of DKK 8,000. more a year than other cash benefits recipients. And who will suffer? Unfortunately, it will be the children. With the ceiling on cash benefits, the government weakens equality in Denmark and at the same time makes everyday life significantly harder for a group of single parents who are already in a difficult situation. We in the Social Democratic Party are ready to shoulder our share of responsibility, and we would also very much like to help the government on its way. We believe that the government needs this in this area, which is why I would just like to remind the government in the end that equality does not come naturally. It is not done with words and analyses alone. So let us get on with the new steps and make the visions a reality for both men and women, because the government certainly has us ready to take our share of responsibility.

ParlaMint-DK_20160329170848	Since Enhedslisten's equal opportunities rapporteur, Mrs Pernille Skipper, cannot be here today, I have the honour of presenting the views of Enhedslisten in relation to the Government's statement on equality. The government has chosen to focus its efforts on gender equality in three areas: individual rights and freedoms, better use of resources and talents, and a global equal opportunities effort. The first category, rights and freedoms of the individual, according to the government, includes the fight against trafficking in human beings, women's oppression among ethnic minorities, the fight against violence in close relationships and everyday sexism. It is positive that the government attaches importance to everyday sexism. The fact that women in Denmark put up with bells, shouts and sexual comments is a problem of equality. And with the recognition that this problem exists, we must also recognise that equality is both a structural and a cultural problem. Equality is not achieved by writing on a piece of paper or in a law that men and women have the same rights. It is also a question of getting a culture in which men and women are equally worthwhile and where women are not treated less because of their gender. That is why we have to recognise that it is an expression of a cultural problem when 32%. of Danish women have experienced violence or sexual abuse in a couple relationship. Approx. 33,000 women are subjected to physical or sexual violence each year, and too many rape cases are not taken seriously enough, too few are solved, and far too few lead to conviction. It is also a cultural problem that, among other things, has to be addressed. in certain minority environments in Denmark is a greater repression of women, more cases of violence and more strong social control than we find in the rest of Denmark. It is therefore very good that the government will continue and expand its work on empowerment, among other things. through girls' groups and finally get a residence offer in Jutland to those for whom the conflicts have come so far that they have to flee in the dark and gloom of the night. We also hope in the Enhedslisten that the government will increase the focus on outpatient crisis relief and dialogue efforts between women and their parents. But there is something missing from the government in their efforts for minority women fighting for liberation. Fortunately, we see a lot of women shouting loudly, demanding respect and saying from the people and we must support them. We do not do this by smearing their religion or cutting everyone over a comb. We do, among other things. by building a strong welfare society that can be their safety net when they take the leap. The parties behind the cash aid reform do not seem to understand that, when they withdraw economic independence under vulnerable women, they also remove their opportunities to get out of violence and control, because only with independence do they have a real opportunity to leave a violent man if you also want a future for yourself and your children. We can hear this from both the women themselves and the shelters. That is why the first thing we should do is to abandon the reform of the social security system and introduce the right to centre of crisis and the right to protection, that is to say. If we believe that we want to combat violence and social control, then we must help to recover. Lack of equality is a cultural thing, yes, and it is about individual rights, yes. But it is certainly also about what conditions the collective offers the individual, so you have a real choice and real opportunity for something else. The second category of government action is about better use of talent, and it covers the inequality between men and women in the education system and in the labour market. It seems crazy that the government, on the one hand, shuts down the special research programme YDUN, which was targeted at women, while, on the other hand, at the same time preaching equality. It is really a pity that in many ways the government does not really move forward from the recognition of the existence of the problem and get started with actual action. When it comes to maternity leave, the Minister writes in his statement: "The regulations for men to take paternity and parental leave are there and the families can now flexibly distribute parental leave as they please. The government wants more men to take a bigger share of the bar. Then there is not really any other effort on the drawing board than more dialog. In the Enhedslisten, we do not understand why the government is opposed to men being given the same maternity leave as women. Today, women are entitled to earmarked maternity leave. Why should men not be granted the corresponding right? It would give fathers the same rights; it would bring us closer to giving children rights to both their mother and their father; and it would help on the massive unequal pay that still exists and that over a long life accumulates, so that women even have less money for old age. Women lose 10% on average. of the salary of each child they receive. That's not fair. Recently, a study showed that at the present rate we will get equal pay in approx. 111 years. Too many people still point out that it is for women's sake: they just have to pull themselves together, get better at negotiating wages, change jobs a little more often or get better at boasting. All the marine explanations are about the individual woman herself being able to change the unequal pay if she changes her behavior, but that is and remains nonsense. Inequality between the sexes is also a structural problem with a general perception that the work of women and girls is not worth as much as that of men, and it does not fix itself. We need to re-establish gender-based wage statistics; in public contracts we need to give the wage-earners a proper boost in the classic women's professions: their work is worth no less because it is caring, on the contrary. This requires, as the Minister himself writes, that all parties take part, and this also applies to the government. I have not had the opportunity to talk about the global imbalance, and that is not good. Because when poverty, war, hunger or natural disasters hit women harder. It is therefore incredible that the government has not mentioned their cuts in foreign aid or the lack of support for the secular forces in Syria and Northern Iraq that are trying to combat ISIS and Assad, or the cuts in green efforts, so that the fight against natural disasters has been reversed. I hope that we will have time to discuss the world's global challenges in a gender perspective independently another time. Thank you for the floor.

The aim must be for all of us, men and women, to have equal opportunities, and the rest must be for each family to decide for themselves. So the government's very clear objective of having more men take maternity leave, the Liberal Alliance does not share, and I would say that I do not understand how a bourgeois-liberal government believes to know that it is good for fathers to take maternity leave, which is actually written literally in the statement. Part of being liberal is, of course, precisely that we recognise the individual and the differences we as human beings have, including different preferences. We cannot and should not comment on what is the good life for adult people. We should create the framework for the individual to make the best choice for himself. We therefore share the aim that the possibility of maternity should be present for the man as well as for the woman, and that possibility exists today. I would also like to mention that I think it is positive that Denmark is a model for other countries. There are many countries that are far, far behind in this area, and if we can in any way contribute to increased equality in these places, I think it is fantastic. That is why we fully support the government's focus on the international contribution. Last I would like to mention that a few months ago I met Jytte Hilden for a talk about equality, and she opened my eyes to how grateful our generation should be for the struggle of previous generations in this field. My foundation has been so solid that I have never looked down and seen that I am actually standing on the shoulders of former cool equality defenders, and they must of course be thankful, and I owe it to my son and a future daughter to ensure that the foundation that has been created is preserved in the future. ParlaMint-DK_20160329173445	Thank you. First of all, I would like to thank the Minister for his statement, which I have read with great interest. Therefore, we would like to invite the government to dig deeper into a spade-stick so that the structures that support the lack of equality are addressed. In line with the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party, who also mentioned something about digging a shovel deeper, we would like to invite you to do so in this debate. One example is that when girls and boys' gender-based educational choices are addressed in the statement, it is established that parents have different expectations of their children's educational choices, which may be part of the problem. It is also pointed out that one of the problems of equality is that boys are professionally lagging behind girls, while girls are well-suited to the boys. It is not a surprise, but it is not addressed that the different expectations of boys and girls start well before primary school. They start, from the children are born, yes, sometimes actually almost before the children are born, because they are institutionalized in our view of child rearing, in our expectations of children, in our practice of upbringing, in schools, in leisure homes, in kindergarten, in the media; all the places where children and young people are confronted with how it is right to be a boy and how it is right to be a girl. So if this problem really needs to be addressed, we must dare to face up to the fact that there are standards, even in Danish society, which very early entangle children and put them in a booth that gives a very narrow definition of their gender. So we only do this by starting at the very bottom of the mass, namely, from where our gender perceptions and our assumptions and oversights come, and that mass is made up of some pretty fixed standards and ideas. A very good example of some who try to address it is the Normstorms, which have projects in the City of Copenhagen and in Aarhus, where they are out in primary schools and teach children and also teachers how to constantly attack the ideas and assumptions that we have in terms of gender and gender perception. I would like to recommend to the government that they take this into account and into the work when setting up a working group to look at this challenge in relation to primary school. Now I've been talking about standards. The only place I can actually read the word'standards' in the statement is in the section dealing with refugee education. I am very happy to work for a Denmark where Dad can easily go home with the children. Such a society would create the best conditions and role models for the new Danes who come to the country, but also for our own future generations. Because if there is something that especially little boys need, it's actually getting some male role models that practice care and show how to be an empathetic being. But there is a difference between rejecting something and trying to understand an action. Perhaps it is not so hard to understand that some young men may find it difficult to find an expression of their frustrations, their anger, their insecurity, their anxiety, their insecurity, when many have grown up without any male role models that are close and actually show how to practice care and how to practice empathy, and how to treat other people empathically and caringly. I know it sounds a little caricatured. I'm not standing up here saying that men are not caring and empathetic, because they certainly are. Therefore, there is also a need for Dad to get more on the field at home. But this means that we must dare to use the tools that create the conditions for the standards we would like to see, such as a father of duty. And an obvious tool, which we have also discussed in this Chamber today, and which we from other countries know to work, is earmarked maternity. The Minister writes, and we have also mentioned this, that the framework for men to take paternity and parental leave is there and that the families today can flexibly distribute parental leave as they please. Yeah, if there was an equal wage, you might say, maybe that was the case. But the fact is that it is still the man who makes the most money and that it is usually the woman who takes the bar. So that's only 20%. by the Danish men who take the maternity leave that can be distributed. In e.g. Iceland has earmarked three months' maternity leave for men out of nine months in total, and that is over 90 per cent. of the fathers who take the 3 months, and many take more than the 3 months. So it works. The SFI did this study, which we have also mentioned, about earmarked maternity leave for men, and it showed that the legislation on earmarking can actually have a normative effect and help normalize that fathers take leave. So why don't we go that way in Denmark? When I've asked fathers in my company I often have, because I'm actually interested in hearing what they really think so many say that I would have liked to have taken more maternity leave, but there was concern for the family's finances and my family's finances could not bear it. And many also say: I would have liked to have taken more maternity leave, but it was a little bit difficult for me to get to my place of work and say it, because it is not very common where I work. So, in reality, there is no freedom in the families to distribute the barsel freely between the parents. That is why we really need to dare to ask ourselves how we are freeing the family to make free choices. For us in the alternative, earmarked maternity is not a duty, it is a right. It is a right to fathers and therefore an increased opportunity for more free elections in families. In the section on refugees there is another thing I feel is necessary to address. The minister writes in the statement that refugees and immigrants must be informed about equality and about the rights of women and sexual minorities in Danish society. We have also mentioned this in the past. Among other things, it is mentioned that the government will implement this mandatory module for training in asylum centres, such as: on these subjects. We are in agreement with that. But if you remember a little bit back on your old Danish teaching and read this section, you quickly realize that everything that says about this is in passive form. It says that refugees and asylum seekers must be provided with information, knowledge, information and so on. That is what I am asking: where is the participation? Where's the involvement? Where is civil society? Where is the link between refugees and Danes? Where is the incentive to involve Danish volunteers in the work of friendship families, homework help, sportsmates etc.? These are people who can actually involve and help refugees and immigrants in practice to be introduced into the norms of gender equality that we have in Denmark. I really miss that. It is our belief in the alternative that equality and respect for women's rights cannot only be learned in a classroom. It must also be learned in practice. That is why we need to think about civil society in these efforts. Then I go back to the standards for a brief comment. The standards of Danish society, which we take so much for granted in terms of gender perception and equality, do not only affect the family. It also shows the section on everyday sexism and sexism in e.g. social media. We need the courage to address that. These are the standards that make it seem natural and inevitable and therefore okay when some men abuse women in the late hours of the night, because did the woman now say no with a voice that was firm enough? It is also the standards that make it difficult for men exposed to violence to seek help. These are the standards that make it difficult to address sexism and racism in the workplace, without destroying the good mood or getting to seem prude. It is also the standards that allow you to see the word bitch or whore in a comment on Facebook, and that comment even gets likes. It is only when we dare face up to the Danish gender standards that we can really do something about the informal lack of equality that permeates Danish society. It is a lack of equality which harms both women and men and all those who do not feel like either or. So, in the alternative, we would really like to have a debate on this, which is digging deeper into a spade, and to use some of the knowledge that exists in this area, which makes it clear not only what the problems are, but also what the problems are due to, and in which we will therefore have the framework for a more thorough debate, but also a bolder debate, because we are all forced to debate the standards that we are all helping to maintain. Then we can have an open debate on whether the root causes of the problems we then identify are something we should address politically. We may disagree on that. But let us take the debate. And by that I would like to thank you for the floor.

Then, together with the other bourgeois parties, the government has voted through a cash aid reform which will increase the inequality of income and opportunities already between the sexes. It particularly affects single parents, three out of four of whom are women. And violence-stricken women and women living in highly socially controlled environments, which are also in focus in this report, will have particular difficulty in meeting the requirements of the 225-hour rule. The key question is whether or not we care about equality. Is this something you see as important in a democratic society and are you willing to legislate for equality, or are you going to let it be a personal and individual matter, that is, something you have to take care of at home and at work? Men and women still have unequal conditions on the labour market. There is still no equal pay for work, although we have an equal pay law and there is an inexplicable pay difference of 4-7 per cent. In the SF, we want the pay gap to be closed both through agreements and through legislation. There must be greater transparency about wage differences in the workplace, and gender-based pay statistics are one of the ways to do so. The relationship between work and family life is also an issue of equality. There is an imbalance in the distribution of responsibilities within the family between fathers and mothers. Although the legislation allows for the sharing of the bar, the fathers end up holding a very small part of it. On average, fathers only last about one month's maternity leave and women for the remaining 10-11 months. According to the London School of Economics and the University of Copenhagen, it costs the woman 10%. of her salary for each child she receives, because of a reduced presence in the labour market and because of the hourly wage. And on average women save only 80 kroner. up to pension for every $100, men save up. It makes them poorer when they retire, and it puts them at a disadvantage in divorce. The government wants more men to take part in the bar, and calls for joint responsibility for equality in the labour market. This requires effort from several, but what responsibility will the government actually take and what expectations it has, for example. to the social partners? SF wants men to have the same right to maternity leave as women, both for the sake of children and for the sake of equality. Another key issue in the report is trafficking in human beings and prostitution, where there is an increase. The action plan places emphasis on outreach work and the fight against backers, and we can only agree with that. However, the statement does not say much about what the government will do to prevent more trade coming. 80% the victims of trafficking in human beings have been subjected to prostitution. Is there a willingness to provide protection for these vulnerable women? Will they be given free passage and residence permits to ensure that they do not just end up in the arms of the backmen? And why should it be legal to buy sex for women and thus create a paradise for the sponsors and a free market for buyers of sexual services? In the SF, we do not want a society where vulnerable women must be available for men's need for sexual satisfaction at the expense of their physical and mental health. And we want a strong focus on rehabilitation and psychological assistance for women in prostitution. I would also like to touch on violence in close relationships. It is good that projects such as "Out of Violence Shadow" and "Dialog Against Violence" have been extended; that there has been a focus on violent men; and that there is an effort in relation to stalking. We support this in the SF, and we did so in the context of the wage-pool negotiations. At the same time, we do not think it is enough to help the victim of the violence. The perpetrators and witnesses of violence, which are often children, also need help if the pattern of violence is to be broken. And we should expand the scope for help. In addition to providing opportunities for shelters, we should also make it a right to receive outpatient treatment for those who do not necessarily need protection, but help to get out of the pattern of violence. The transition from the shelter back to home can be difficult, and the risk of falling back into old patterns is great. Therefore, there should be some form of mandatory post-protection where the crisis centres do not let go of contact. The government seems to think that things are going well without legislative action. We don't share that patience with the SF. Greater political responsibility must be taken. We are not in line with equality, and equality does not come naturally. The report lists a lot of relevant issues, and the SF hopes to be called for negotiations in terms of ways of equal pay, a maternity fund for self-employed workers, a more equal distribution of maternity leave, discrimination against barren and pregnant women, better efforts in relation to violence, social control, harassment, hatefulness and everyday sexism. There are a lot of things to do. Similarly, we also hope that the government will take concrete initiatives to combat trafficking in human beings for prostitution, which effectively protect vulnerable women. But thank you for your statement. There's enough to do.

In October last year, the 22-year-old Ditte Brink Kammersgaard was named one of Denmark's best mechanic apprentices. She was one woman among the recipients of the prize and one of the few women on mechanic training. Ditte had largely gone against the flow when she chose education, for it takes today in her woman to become a mechanic, just as it takes her husband to become a crèche teacher. I would never dream of making choices for the young. I would never dream of saying that girls should always choose one type of education or boys should go for what has traditionally been girl education. I think it is important that we keep an eye on the fact that there are still some subjects that special boys or girls choose, not necessarily because they could not dream of a career in the other subject, but because they do not feel at home in the studies because they have no role models to reflect in. When we, on the part of the government and the Left, stress that equality is very much about freedoms, rights and opportunities, then it is also about ensuring that individuals, whether it be about educational choices, life choices, partner for life or a number of other areas, have the opportunity to make the choices that suit the individual. Whether you're a girl or a boy, you shouldn't be the one that inhibits your options and hampers your choices in life. Equality is a central right of freedom in our society. It is about everyone having equal opportunities to unfold their potentials and talents like Ditte, as I mentioned before. All resources must be at stake and no one should be discriminated against on the basis of their gender. Equality does not mean that we should be the same. That doesn't mean we have to get fifty-fifty distribution of everything. In my universe equality rhymes not with coercion and regulation, but with freedoms and opportunities. The key point is that equality offers freedoms and opportunities for the individual: freedom to choose an unconventional education, to go part-time as a man to be with his children or to get to the top of business as a woman. Equality is also about the right to decide over one's own body and life. The right to have a life free from violence, free from stalking, free from being traded for exploitation in another country, freedom to marry whoever you want. Equality is basically to show respect for each other and to ensure that men and women are equally worthless. As the debate today has also shown, and as several rapporteurs have also said, unfortunately in parts of Danish society we find that the equal value of men and women, the right to choose one's own partner for life, the right, of course, to live a life without violence or without social control, is unfortunately not the reality of certain people in Danish society. We have parallel societies in some places in the country where women have no right to choose the partner they want. For that matter, boys do not necessarily have the right to choose the partner they want to spend their lives with. Women do not have the opportunity to choose education, get a job, be active in society, both imams and their men oppress them, and the family imposes conventions and rules which do not correspond to the rules and freedoms we have in society, but which are largely driven by norms and culture, social control and parallel societies. I believe that the events in Cologne New Year's Night for a lot of people were a wakeup call compared to the reverse of the large migratory flows we are seeing in Europe at the moment. We need to be extremely aware that when there are many people coming to our society and to Europe, we also need to insist that the great progress and the objectives we have achieved in the area of equality are not being undermined. We will not accept retrograde steps explained or explained away from religion, culture, social background or anything else. Now many of the rapporteurs were in New York just before Easter to the United Nations Women's Commission and the Women's Commission meeting. Perhaps they also helped to remember us in many ways, both how far we have come in Denmark, how much we are a pioneer country in the field of equality, but also how many battles need to continue at global level to ensure equality. Somehow it also helped to emphasize how wonderful it is to live in a society like the Danish one, where, whether you are called Jens or Jette, you are born equal and have the same opportunities and rights in our society. That is why we must also insist that this also applies, be it Mohammed or Sahima. There are equal opportunities and equal rights in Danish society. That is why we also want the government to ensure that people who come here, even when they come to the asylum centres, are informed about what equality is in Denmark. What are these rights, what are these values, what kind of culture, what kind of expectations do we have of newcomers? It is basic information about equality and the freedoms that apply in the country, but it is also to highlight that here you have both the right to choose the partner you want for life, but also the opportunity to choose the education you want, and getting a job is a very good way to integrate into Danish society. Being active in the Danish culture of association, whether you are a boy or a girl, is a very important place to create friendships and create some of the networks that help to integrate you into Danish society. We must never accept abuses and abuses explained away on the basis of religion or on the basis of culture. That is why it is important that we stand together to insist on equality and the values, including in these years when many people come to our country. Violations and abuse are never women's fault. All explanations about culture and ethnicity or demands for change in behaviour for women are simple attempts not to want to take absolute and complete exception to any abuse against women. Unfortunately, there are still thousands of women in Denmark who live with violence in everyday life, who have violence in the family and close relationships. It is violence that also affects their children. That is why we have also chosen from the government to step up our efforts and refocus some of the resources in the violence action plan, so that we have more focused and effective efforts to combat violence. We also chose to step up efforts to combat lovers' violence, because if we are to break the language of violence and ensure that men and women do not take violence into close relationships and relationships, then we also have to deal with it when it is the one who is a little innocent flirt among young people who then develop into social control and possessism in matters of what one's boyfriend may or may not do. It's also about stalking. We have up to 130 000 Danes who are subjected to stalking each year, and it has to be said that women are twice as likely to be subjected to stalking as men. This is why the government has taken a number of initiatives in the field of stalking, because stalking also, as a form of violence, is helping to destroy lives and people's ability to live the life they want. The Minister of Justice and the government also took an initiative earlier this year in the field of rape, because we can still see that when it comes to combating rape and ensuring that victims have proper legal certainty, there are still places where we can improve. It is not acceptable for a young girl to come and report a rape, the first of which is faced with a question of what clothes she was wearing. That is why we also need, and this is also what we are doing with the perspective and action plan and with the concrete initiatives taken by the government, to address the places where we have real equality problems still in our society. The government has also chosen to strengthen its efforts towards victims of trafficking in human beings. Unfortunately, the previous government cut down on efforts to combat trafficking in human beings. We have chosen to strengthen the outreach work for victims dealt with forced labour and prostitution. We have also chosen to ensure that the outreach work is again nationwide and more uniform across the country. Because we will not accept the kind of modern slavery that human trafficking is. Everyone has the right to control his own body and his own life. This also applies to the people who are being dealt with here. We must take action against the cynical backers who exploit unhappy souls every day, both for prostitution, but also for forced labour and financial crime. As Minister of Education, several of the rapporteurs have also mentioned this, I have also addressed some of the gender differences and gender equality challenges in the field of school and education. Young people's educational choices remain very gender-based, where you can see that in schools the boys are professionally lagging behind, so we can see that very many of the girls are miserable and struggling with problems of well-being and self-esteem. We must ensure that both boys and girls have equal opportunities, including at school. This applies in professional terms, it applies to well-being. It shouldn't be the case that our primary school's academic results, or whether you're thriving, depend on whether you're Oliver or you're called Olivia. It must be the case that there is an equal opportunity as a child to achieve the goals and have the potentials and dreams that you have. There are some rapporteurs who, like us, have suggested that we on the part of the government will decide how families should distribute the bar. We don't want to. We do not want that in any way. We believe that it is up to the individual family to decide with themselves how to distribute the bar, but we can see from studies that a great many men, despite having the opportunities today, point out that they (1) do not know what rights they have and (2) that they really want to take more maternity leave than what they end up with, among other things. because there is a labour market culture which sometimes means that there is not as much focus on what male employees' maternity conditions look like compared to those of female employees. I think we should be ready to take that discussion in good dialogue with business and the social partners on how we can help to ensure that the choices of each family are really free and not determined by the lack of knowledge about their rights and opportunities, or by the lack of understanding that you actually have the rights and opportunities to decide as a family for yourself how to prioritise and how to divide the bars between you. There is no need for legislation in any way, and I believe that we must say that there may well be a need for a boost both to the workplace culture that may be in some places, but also to a boost for men to engage more in the important part of being a father and to engage in the first part of your child's lifetime. We are a pioneering country in the field of equality, and Denmark must continue to make its mark globally. We need to push on developments. We must demand some of the same things we demand at home, including on the international stage. We must insist that equality and women's rights should be on the international agenda. The creation of global growth and development also requires that the half of the population that is oppressed in a number of countries, namely women, be given a place equal to that of men. That is why I and the government are very proud that Denmark is hosting two important international events this May: Women Deliver, the largest conference on women's rights in the world, and also the IDAHO Forum, which focuses on women's rights in the world. The rights of homosexuals. Because it is important that we also help to take the lead on us to show what we have done in Denmark, what we continue to do, why are we never going to accept inequality between men and women? Both of these major international events are opened up by the Crown Princess, and a large number of ministers from Denmark and abroad will participate, EU institutions and organisations and a large number of players from both Denmark and abroad. I also hope that the parliamentary rapporteurs will engage in the events and engage in the activities of the IDAHO Forum and the Women Deliver Conference, because I really think that it is also an important signal to take the debate we had today to send out the clear signal that in Denmark equality is something we agree on. Yes, there may be nuance differences, but basically we agree that equality and the equal value of men and women are the starting point for the society that we have. For me and for the government, equality is a struggle for freedom and rights. It is global, and it is in Denmark. We must safeguard equality and we must continue to work to ensure that everyone has equal opportunities to exploit their potential and freedom to choose the life they want to live. The government is taking the lead in this, and with this perspective and action plan we have also set out the priorities for the coming year. But the government cannot and must not lift the gender equality agenda alone. For equality is a shared responsibility. There is a need for many people to push forward. This applies to the individual girl and boy, man and woman, but it also applies to families, businesses, educational institutions and organisations and to us as politicians. So when the SF's rapporteur says that the government just wants to make equality the kind of thing that the individual has to mess with, I can say no. As we have also written in the perspective and action plan, the fight for equality remains a joint struggle. It's also a fight the government would like to take. Thank you for the good comments. I look forward to future cooperation in the field of equal opportunities, and I hope that the commitment that has been made in this Chamber today will also be a characteristic of the initiatives we will have to take in the future to achieve the objectives we have set ourselves in the area of equality.
