ParlaMint-DK_20160329192705	Thank you, sir. President. On 3 December 2015, as you know, the Danes went to the ballot boxes to vote on Denmark's reservation of law. Almost 54 percent. of the Danes voted against lifting the reservation and thereby leaving the competence to surrender sovereignty in the field of justice to a majority at Christiansborg. The Danes thus once again cemented that there is a massive opposition to further EU integration. Changing governments have been aware since the entry into force of the Treaty of Lisbon that there was no challenge for Denmark in relation to continued membership of Europol when Europol became supranational. In the meantime, however, all governments have failed to ensure that Denmark continues to become a member of Europol, but instead expected Europol to be the lever needed to secure the Danes' agreement to abolish the legal reservation. As you know, this did not succeed, which is why it is now time to move on from there. It has now been a long time since we voted on the reservation of the law, and also since we tabled this question. We in the Danish People's Party have had a great deal of sympathy for the fact that after a heated election campaign the government could not suddenly jump out of line with a fixed and final agreement on a Danish parallel agreement on Europol, but we must also say that now it is gradually beginning to be time for something to happen. That is why this question comes at the right time and we look forward to hearing what the Minister can tell us about the forthcoming process towards the conclusion of an agreement.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329192853	Yes, and it is precisely the Minister who now has the opportunity to answer the question.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329192859	On 3 December 2015, as I said, a referendum was held on whether Denmark should transform its current legal reservation into an electoral system in which Denmark could choose on a case-by-case basis which legal acts Denmark wanted to participate in. I am not going to go into detail here today in this debate about what a transformation of the legal reservation would have given of opportunities for the Danish Government in the legal field. We have had that debate and, as far as I remember, we have had quite thorough discussions with regard to the legislative proposal L 29. I should just like to say here, which is not a great surprise, that the referendum ended with a majority of the Danes not being able to add, however, that, to me, the question of the transformation of the legal reservation was not a question of more EU, but a question of Denmark having the right to choose which parts of the European Union we want to be in, and that the fact that we received a 'no' means that in some areas we stand to cooperate less with the other EU countries, perhaps even in some of the areas where we most need a common European response to the challenges of time. In the period before and after the referendum, it has become apparent that the government and the other parties in this Chamber are all of the opinion that what the Danes voted against was not Europol, but the context of that. It was the question of the scope of the transformation of the reservation and the possibilities it would offer in order to be able to choose other things. That was the question of the other 22 specific acts, etc. etc. However, because there was very clear support for the fact that, regardless of the 'no' vote in the referendum, the Danes had not said no to Europol, the Prime Minister contacted the President of the European Commission and the President of the European Council on the evening of the vote to inform them of the outcome of the vote. This was followed up by a meeting of the Prime Minister, the President of the European Commission and the President of the European Council as early as the week after the referendum. At home there have also been a large number of political contacts to find a solution to how to move forward. The Prime Minister has had bilateral meetings with all parties; three party leader meetings have been held and so far these meetings have resulted in an agreement to request a parallel agreement with Europol. This was the discussion that took place during the referendum itself, namely whether converting the reservation into an electoral system or seeking a parallel agreement was the best way for Denmark to maintain our cooperation and participation in Europol. As there was agreement to request a parallel agreement, we did so on 20 January 2016. There is still no agreement as to whether we in Denmark should ask for parallel agreements in other courts in the field of cross-border crime prevention. It could be the so-called PNR, i.e. a passenger register of air travellers to Europe; it could be Eurojust; it could be the search warrant. It is all three pieces of legislation that are important for the work of the police. In particular, I think it is a shame, given these days and the importance that a PNR register would have as an important tool in the fight against terrorism, that we do not have the opportunity to choose for ourselves here in the Folketing whether or not we want to participate in a PNR register. But this will be one of the issues on which we need to have a thorough discussion, in other words whether we should seek a form of participation in the future, a kind of parallel agreement in that area too. Among other things: Therefore, on 11 February, I held a meeting with the Minister of Justice and the EU rapporteurs on this issue. I do not think it is an exaggeration to say that there was no clear direction or clear conclusion as to what might be desired in relation to the question of further parallel agreements. We therefore agreed that the parties should have more information about the acts and that we should then hold a new meeting. We also agreed that we would go on a joint study trip to Brussels to talk with the Commission, with the European Parliament, with the specific key people who are there. That is why we had planned a trip here earlier in March, which unfortunately had to be cancelled. This was because some of the key people with whom we had to meet in order to have the relevant gravity in such a meeting were engaged in dealing with the refugee and migration crisis that Europe has been in and where some of the same people have been crucial to the negotiations that have taken place, both within the EU circle and also with external partners. I would very much like to return to you with a new invitation as soon as possible, because I think it is important that we take such a trip together, so that we have a common reference basis, get a joint presentation and also jointly get presented what the Danish position is, so it is not just the government's position, but positions that cover the Danish Parliament's parties. Once we have been in Brussels, we need to follow up quickly at home and discuss the other pieces of legislation. So when the trip to Brussels is in place and complete, I will come back with an invitation for a closer discussion. I hope that the parties will then be in a situation where they can come a little closer to clear reports, clear positions, in relation to what they might think of any other legal acts, for which it may also be necessary to apply for a parallel agreement. I would like to say quite quietly that we in the government have made it clear throughout the process that parallel agreements are not something you can just pull in an EU machine. This requires the Commission's approval, it requires the approval of the European Parliament, and it requires the approval of the other 27 Member States. The experience we have in this area and we have of course several times sought, sometimes received approval, sometimes received refusal of parallel agreements • shows that such an application takes time and that our wish is not always met. The second thing we know is that, from the outset, the government has made it clear that the message from Brussels is that a parallel agreement cannot be negotiated into a legal act until the act is finally adopted. And that means that the answer to the question, whether we could not have just started a discussion on a parallel agreement with Europol something earlier, is that we could not do so until Europol had finally been adopted. Now it has finally been adopted by the Council of Ministers; it must then be confirmed by the European Parliament, and I assume it will be. But others present with more experience from the European Parliament than I can perhaps say whether it is likely to be confirmed. I think it will be confirmed and thus adopted. Only after that has happened will we have the legal basis on which we can start to make the very formal steps that need to be taken by applying for a parallel agreement, by clarifying the position of the Commission, the Council, Parliament and the EU countries on whether they want to make a parallel agreement, and then starting the negotiations themselves on it. This means that we do not actually have much time to get a parallel agreement in place. That does not mean that we have given it up in any way. We will work even hard and determined to ensure that Denmark is also part of Europol after May 2017. It will be in the interest of the Danes and it will be in everyone's interest. I therefore hope that everyone in this Chamber will be helpful in the process that is coming. Thank you.

The third thing is that if the government wants a new referendum on 3 December, the government must have an ambition to find other parties that will support the government. There are certainly no other parties against the government in the referendum of 3 December which will be prepared to go out and change their position on the same basis. I can't imagine.

ParlaMint-DK_20160329205543	Thank you, sir. President. It is now almost time since the Danish People's Party announced this inquiry debate. ParlaMint-DK_20160329205814	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then I give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs to answer the question.
ParlaMint-DK_20160329205821	At the European Council of 18 and 19 February this year, agreement was reached on an agreement addressing the wishes for reform of EU cooperation raised by the British Prime Minister in his letter of 15 November 2015. To start a step further back, there has been a long way to go since Cameron announced that, in the event of election victory, he would ask for a renewed referendum in Britain on their association with the European Union and for a dialogue with a large number of people, including governments, including, of course, the Danish Government, on what kind of framework such a renewed agreement was within. That is why we knew in advance that there would be four points in which Britain and David Cameron would ask for changes in the relationship. These are questions of solutions in areas relating to the following: (1) euro countries and non-euro countries; (2) competitiveness and better regulation; (3) the issue of sovereignty, not least an enhanced role for national parliaments; (4) a special British interpretation of what was already clarified in 2014: how, as the Treaty of Rome states, the concept of ever closer union should be understood in a British context. So these have been four areas in which there has been some progress before and a long dialogue on the eve of the European Council on 18 and 19 February. The agreement also contains solutions in the area of welfare and freedom of movement. This is among other things. has been agreed that it is possible to index the children's cheque to EU citizens in the future, whose children do not live in the same country as the parent who has earned the allowance. It was also agreed that the much-mentioned Metockdom of 2008 will be rolled back. It is no secret that this government has been very forceful throughout the whole process from the warning that Britain will be asking for a change, and during the discussions up to Cameron's letter of 10 November, and not least during the period up to the summit, has strongly supported Britain's efforts in the negotiations. There are many reasons for this: Britain and Denmark have an historically close relationship, we came into the European Union at the same time and in the same age, we have followed along and had common priorities along the way in order to ensure that the European Union was as effectively managed with as much focus on competitiveness and job creation as possible, and we often look at things the same way. Therefore, it is also the case that Britain and Denmark have worked closely together to get the individual parts of the agreement on Great Britain in place. Many of the things that Britain has got into their agreement are things that Denmark has wanted for a long time. Nor should I conceal the fact that, from a Danish point of view, an EU without Britain will be another EU, it will be a weakened EU, it will be an EU without the same economic capacity, without the same cultural diversity, and it will be an EU without the same political force in terms of being able to speak with a common voice. That is why it has also been important for the government to be able to help to land an agreement that can form the basis for a vote in Britain which can be won. The British Prime Minister thinks he can do that with this agreement, and I hope so too. I don't want to hide the fact that this has been a very difficult fight. All the discussions at the summit on 18 and 19 February were very intense and with very different positions, not only from those parties that wanted to help Britain find an agreement that met the objectives set by Cameron, but also from a number of countries that wanted to move in the opposite direction, which wanted to remove some of the things that were actually on the table and which wanted to water down the basis of the agreement. Therefore, it was a battle in which a large number of countries discussed back and forth, and where it was also important that Denmark was on the field in relation to the rules that apply, for example. for indexing the children's cheque, for Britain, if they vote yes, so that it will be possible for other countries, including Denmark, to bring those rules into the legislation forward-looking. From the Danish point of view, it managed to hold onto the achievements of the negotiating table, and from the Danish point of view, it is a very good agreement with many good elements that provide solutions to issues that we have fought for ourselves. This applies not least to the possibility of indexing the children's cheque. It has, of course, been a Danish wish and wish expressed by the government on several occasions, so that it should be the case that the payment that is being made is of course intended to cover the additional costs of having children and that the sum paid must reflect the actual costs of the country in which the child is staying. This is about the roll-back of the Metock judgment, on which we have disagreed over all the years from 2008 onwards, as far as the conclusions are concerned, and we, on our part, have worked to change the rulebook behind the judgment that was taken so that a new decision can be taken. I am also very pleased with the result in relation to relations between euro countries and non-euro countries. I myself have had to raise this issue at the meeting of the Ministers of European Affairs some time ago. The euro countries must, of course, have the opportunity to meet on what is relevant to the euro area countries. However, where it is relevant to all 28, it is the 28 countries that are to meet, not the euro countries, which, as it were, meet on their own. I am also pleased that the principles here have been put in place. That is why the result that has been achieved, which is now to be voted on in Britain, is well along the road and in the direction that the government would like the European Union to move forward. Nor do I believe that we have finished the discussion on the basis now, among other things. the question of the rights of migrant workers, what is called Regulation 883. This is a regulation that we have asked the Commission to propose to amend to ensure that it is possible to have the right to travel as a worker, but not to travel for welfare benefits. It is a regulation that has not been negotiated in the context of the agreement that lies for Britain here, but a regulation that needs to be worked on in the future to ensure that you can travel to work, but not to travel for welfare benefits. Now it is up to the British people in a referendum to decide whether they want to become part of European work or whether they want to leave the European Union. And that is important not only for Britain but for the whole of Europe, not only because, as I said, a Europe without Britain will be another Europe, a weaker Europe, a weakened Europe in a large number of areas, but also because the elements with which we in Denmark are very satisfied are contained in the agreement, will only come into force if it becomes a 'yes' in Great Britain. It is the case that part of the agreement is bound up with those elements, for example. The indexing of children's cheques alone can happen if it becomes a yes, and therefore there is every reason for Danish to look forward to the vote with excitement. I do not want to interfere in the votes of other countries, just as I do not expect other countries to interfere in Danish referendums, but, as I think most will be clear, the government hopes that Britain will vote in favour of the European Union. This will allow us to benefit from the elements of the agreement, but it will also ensure, and perhaps more generally, that the EU will be able to speak with the strongest common voice in the future.

The Skepsis vis-à-vis the EU is pronounced throughout Northern and Eastern Europe and in broad parts of Western Europe, as recent elections in inter alia. Slovakia and Germany have, of course, given their views on the matter. If confidence in the European Union is to be rebuilt, the European Union must fundamentally change its structure. Why should the EU dictate what social benefits migrant workers are entitled to in the Member States? The first opportunity to make a fundamental change of course is in relation to the forthcoming negotiations on reform of the regulation on the coordination of the social security systems of the Member States of the European Union, the so-called Regulation 883, in which it is expected by the Danish People's Party that there will be a significant reduction in what migrant workers are entitled to, and a fundamental debate should be opened on when they are to be regarded as migrant workers, so that we get away from the situation that 9 hours of weekly work qualifies one as a migrant worker and thus, for example, as a recipient of the Danish SU. Within a reasonable period of time, the EU's Treaty basis will have to be revised. This opportunity must be used to launch an ambition for a fundamental reform of the European Union, in which more tasks are left to the nation states and where nation states are endowed with the necessary power to stop unnecessary intervention by the European Union. If there is also to be binding European cooperation in ten years' time, then that is what needs to be done. ParlaMint-DK_20160329214037	As I said earlier, in the alternative, we are always happy to have a more in-depth discussion of what we want with European cooperation. For European work is greater than what we have seen in recent years. The need to take up this fundamental discussion can hardly be emphasised enough. The European Union faces major global challenges, which require much better answers than those agreed so far by the Member States. Today, European cooperation is extremely challenging. The refugee crisis, the climate crisis and the very high youth unemployment are all cross-border crises requiring collective responses. If we in the European Union fail to sit down and have the difficult discussions on how to improve the European Union, then we run the risk of the refugee crisis getting even more out of hand because we did not dare to use the necessary tools. We risk losing the fight against climate change and pushing huge bills on to our children and grandchildren. We risk losing an entire generation on the floor because we were unable to give young people the opportunities they deserve while they were in time. That is why it is high time for us in the European Union to come together and have the political courage to dare European cooperation, even when the challenges seem great. Reform of EU cooperation should therefore not result in closing ourselves down, but rather in a united and progressive European Union, so that we can once again lead the way in global reforms that can bring about a serious, sustainable transformation and a positive change at global level. This change requires both political priority and investment, and not least it requires leadership from progressive countries of which Denmark could be one. The alternative vision of EU cooperation is crystal clear: we want to draw Europe in a more sustainable direction. This applies at both economic, social and environmental levels. However, this also applies to the spread of human rights and equality, where we in Denmark have used our vote in the past to take the lead. In the alternative, we do not want a reform of the European Union to take the example of the agreement between Britain and the European Union. Britain's agreement with the European Union does not solve the deep-rooted issues of European cooperation. We believe that a reform of cooperation should focus on strengthening democracy and cooperation and creating more transparency in the European institutions. Nor do we believe that a reform of the European Union based on the points of the agreement will solve the great crisis of confidence facing the European Union in relation to the people of Europe. This will probably have the opposite effect. Let me give just a few examples. Many people have said that we do not want the European Union to develop into a social union, and it is proposed that special suspension arrangements can be put in place with the English agreement to try to stem what has been very ugly called welfare tourism. Firstly, I still believe that we have not seen any evidence that there are real problems with welfare tourism. Secondly, I fear that with measures such as these, we are unnecessarily helping to suspect the motives of some European citizens in order to come to other European countries. With measures such as these, we can help to put our core values at risk, because it is freedom of movement that is the cornerstone of European cooperation and we need migrant workers, including in Denmark. Instead of supporting an over-simple myth formation on welfare tourism, I think we should look at the challenges we really face. Another major focus of Britain's agreement was the focus on the EU's competitiveness. This great and often narrow focus on national economic gains from European cooperation seems to me to be a real shame. This is a shadow of the fact that the European Union is much more than a growth project. The European Union was, and is, above all, a community of solidarity and a peace project that even has the potential to become a lever for global green conversion. So let me come to what the alternative means that a reform of European cooperation will have to contain. In the alternative, we usually say that Denmark must be the best country for the world. It is the same that applies to the European Union. European cooperation must be the best international cooperation for the world, a cooperation that dares to show political courage to take the tough decisions and take the lead in the fight for climate and human rights, for example. We also need to address the democratic deficit in European cooperation, the deficit that feeds popular distrust and creates a problematic gap between citizens and the European institutions. We need to open up decision-making processes even more so that the citizens of the European Union do not feel powerless and without influence. A number of measures could easily be taken to meet this. It only requires political will. For example, a proposal could be to livestream meetings of the Council.

It is bound to be a very hypothetical discussion that we are entering into, and it does not really make any sense until 23 June, so I think there will really be grounds for discussing the consequences, especially if the British say no. The realization that there is something wrong should hit everyone, whether you are an opponent, skeptics or jubilee Europeans, because the house you are building now has no foundation on which to stand. And if the foundation that is, after all, is becoming increasingly porous, countries are falling away, countries are not going to stand up to being defeated, and the people feel abandoned by the European Union because the EU has not acted in relation to the immigrant crisis and the threat of terrorism, then the last remnant of support for that project will disappear. So the jubilee Europeans run the risk of their house falling apart, and we skeptics want to see another European Union that is more democratic and transparent and that has better room for nation states. If it all fucks up, it is bad for us too, because what about the things that are good in EU cooperation, for example. free trade or part of cooperation within Europol? So I just think it is better to engage constructively in working for a better European Union, an EU that can generate popular support, because it is an EU that is not above the Member States but in agreement with the Member States. I think it would be wise to take stock of it, and I think it would be wise for the so-called 'yes' parties to take the temperature of reality and to note that there is a very long way to go from the European enthusiasm represented in this Parliament and the growing dissatisfaction of the population. The answer to the EU's challenges is no longer the European Union. The answer is another EU. And if the question remains for the citizens whether we should have more EU, then the answer will also be no, thank you. It's as safe as the amen in the church. I had hoped that today we could take the lead in using the British vote constructively to point forward and to play along the line that David Cameron has been setting up, but unfortunately I have to admit that I have been disappointed, and I believe that the citizens who have followed this debate behind the screen have also been disappointed. Denmark must be completely behind the British reform efforts now and in the future, and we must do so first and foremost for our own sake.
